Structured caregiver tasks scaffold engagement by construction, since the task instructs the dyad to cooperate, which is precisely the mechanism that compressed the engagement codes in the present case study (Section 4.3). Step 2 applies directly: a withdrawal code the task procedurally prevents should be redefined or dropped before piloting rather than reported as a floor effect afterwards.

In each case, the protocol's output is the same: a per-code table with variance and threshold distance, an explicit list of degenerate and fragile codes, and a calibrated statement of what the pilot could and could not have detected.

What transfers is the procedure and the reporting requirement. The numerical defaults do not transfer without recalibration. The applied stakes of skipping this screen are concrete. If an intervention trial deploys an unscreened composite outcome built the same way as the NSI, a degenerate or near-degenerate withdrawal code could silently cancel against a saturated engagement code in the summary score, so the trial would report a null effect that reflects the instrument's architecture rather than the intervention's true impact; or, in the reverse case, a spurious effect driven by a single unstable code rather than a genuine group difference. Either error could lead a research team, funder, or service commissioner to wrongly discontinue an effective intervention or to invest further in an ineffective one, based on a composite that was never capable of carrying the signal it was assumed to carry. This is precisely why Step 8 of the protocol (Table 1), which calibrates the screen itself against a no-pathology null, and Step 9, the reporting obligation that refuses to substitute a larger pilot for code redesign, are the two safeguards that would catch this failure before it reaches a funded trial's primary analysis.

1. Introduction

Brief, structured laboratory encounters between strangers, most prominently the Fast Friends Procedure (FFP; Aron et al., 1997), are increasingly paired with live-observation sheets that supplement self-report. Such sheets tally observed behaviours per dyad and sum them into a behavioural profile. The usual quality check is distributional: does the summary score look healthy? Yet, a quieter failure mode receives little methodological attention: individual codes can saturate and floor in opposite directions while the summary tally still appears well distributed.

The contribution of this paper is not the existence of ceiling or floor effects, which are well known, but the demonstration that aggregation can conceal opposing code-level failures in live, single-coder observational data where retrospective psychometric correction is not possible.

Such failure may arise whenever a coding scheme mixes high-base-rate cooperative behaviours with low-base-rate distress markers within scaffolded tasks, including conflict-dyad coding, parent-child tasks, and clinical observation.

Our first claim is therefore that a live-observation summary tally can look well distributed whilst its constituent codes are simultaneously saturated and floored, masking instrument failure that only becomes visible at the code level. Section 3 demonstrates this in a real dataset, and Section 4 explains why it happened. We note at the outset that the existence of a ceiling effect in this instrument is not a discovery of the present paper: it is documented in the parent study's own deposited appendices, which record that "a ceiling effect was observed across most dyads, particularly on the Relational subscale, limiting the measure's sensitivity" (de Blauwe, 2026, Appendix I). What is new here is the code-level decomposition of that effect, the demonstration that the composite's apparent health is produced by it, and the simulation reported below.

Our second claim addresses a problem created by the first. Once such a pathology is documented, the natural next question for any researcher designing a new observation sheet is practical: how large a pilot is needed to catch it in advance? Earlier statements of this diagnostic answered with a number, N = 4-8 dyads, extrapolated from the fact that the pathology was visible in the full sample rather than demonstrated. Section 5 supplies the missing test and finds that the question as posed was malformed, and Section 6 rewrites the heuristic accordingly. This exploratory instrument-diagnostic analysis does not test intervention efficacy: its two evidential targets are whether a healthy-looking summary tally can conceal code-level failure, and what minimum pilot size, if any, is needed to detect that concealment. The reasons these principles extend beyond this dataset are addressed directly in Section 6.

2. The Parent Trial and the Instrument

>The Parent Trial and the Instrument

2.1 The Parent Trial, Reported in Full Elsewhere

The data underlying this paper come from a pre-registered, dyad-allocated feasibility trial (AsPredicted #265934; institutional ethics reference UEL2026AGAF, with approved amendment UEL 09012026 AGAF) in which 30 students formed 15 stranger dyads and completed either a brief communication-scaffold primer (Nonviolent Communication; n = 8 dyads) or a timematched body-focused primer (mindfulness; n = 7 dyads) before a 14-minute structured reciprocal conversation.

Recruitment, rather than delivery, was the binding feasibility constraint: conversion from intake to an attended session was 44-52% against a registered 80% threshold. The trial's primary candidate outcome, immediate dyad-level interpersonal closeness, its exact label-permutation tests on 14 complete dyads with a 15-dyad sensitivity check, and its full feasibility-progression table are reported, interpreted, and defended in a companion manuscript, to which the reader is directed for all inferential claims about the intervention comparison (de Blauwe & Fidalgo, 2026a). This paper does not reproduce that Results section, does not restate its permutation statistics, and draws no conclusion about intervention efficacy. The only feature of the parent trial relevant here is that a single researcher completed a ten-code live-observation sheet in real time across all 15 dyads, and that the dyad-by-code totals aggregated from those binlevel sheets were openly deposited: that deposit is the exclusive empirical substrate for this paper.

2.2 Disclosures

Pre-registration scope. The parent trial was pre-registered on AsPredicted (#265934), with feasibility as its primary objective and closeness, belonging, and autonomic regulation as exploratory efficacy outcomes. The NSI was not named in that registration: its deployment, the code-level diagnostic in Section 3, and the Monte Carlo simulation in Section 5 are all unregistered, emerged from inspection of the open data, and are offered as exploratory and hypothesis-generating. The margin index in Section 5.9 and the composite decomposition in Supplementary Material S6 were added at the same stage and are likewise exploratory. Per-code distributions are presented in full in Table 2, with no codes excluded.

Ethical approval. The parent feasibility trial received institutional ethics approval from the University of East London School of Psychology Research Ethics Committee (reference UEL2026AGAF). Written informed consent was obtained from all 30 participants prior to participation, and the research was conducted in accordance with the Declaration of Helsinki.

Reporting. We report how the sample size was determined, all data exclusions (none), all manipulations, and all measures: no observations were excluded for the code-level analysis or for the simulation.

Correction of earlier drafts. Earlier drafts circulated detection-power and maxima values later found to be in error; see Section 5.8 and Appendix C for the full account of what was wrong, how it was found, and what changed.

Patient and public involvement (PPI).

There was no formal PPI panel or lay advisory group for this feasibility trial, consistent with its status as a supervised undergraduate dissertation project rather than a funded programme grant, and we state that absence explicitly rather than omitting the section. We note, distinct from PPI proper, that the participant-facing Welcome Pack and the internal Researcher Laboratory Manual were both revised across pilot sessions in response to issues encountered in early dyads, and these revisions are documented in the study's running pilot log rather than reconstructed after the fact. This iterative refinement reflects standard piloting practice and researcher responsiveness to early-session issues; it does not constitute patient or public involvement in the sense of lay co-design of the research question, outcome measures, or study conduct, and we do not characterise it as such. We record it here only as evidence that piloting fed back into study materials, and flag PPI in the formal sense as absent and as an area for the parent trial programme to address in any future iteration.

Registration status of the screening protocol itself. Table 1's pre-piloting screening protocol is presented as a reusable, instrument-agnostic instrument, and readers should note that it does not carry its own pre-registration or independently timestamped deposit separate from the parent trial's AsPredicted registration (#265934), which registered the trial's feasibility, selfreport, and physiological outcomes but neither the NSI nor the diagnostic protocol derived from it. The protocol was formulated after, and in direct response to, inspection of the deposited NSI data (Section 5.8), and we state this openly rather than implying a timestamp that does not exist: the protocol should be read as post hoc and hypothesis-generating in the same sense as the codelevel diagnostic and simulation it derives from (Section 2.2, Pre-registration scope), and its claim to generality rests on the transferability argument in the introduction and Section 7.4 rather than on prospective registration.

Data, materials, and code. The de-identified dyad-by-code matrix, the codebook and data dictionary, the condition-label correction log, a single executed R script, every output table and figure, Supplementary Tables S1, S1b, S2, S5, S6a, and S6b, and Supplementary Material S6 are openly deposited at OSF (de Blauwe & Fidalgo, 2026b) under CC BY 4.0. Verbatim written debrief responses constitute raw qualitative data from a small, identified sample (N = 30) and cannot be publicly deposited: they are available from the corresponding author on reasonable request, subject to a data access agreement.

2.3 What the NSI Is and Is Not

The NSI is a structured live-observation sheet rather than a psychometric scale: its ten codes count observed behaviours per dyad over a 14-minute interaction, and the summary tally is a weighted sum of those counts. Standard scale-validity statistics, internal consistency and factor structure are, therefore, not the appropriate diagnostics and were not estimable in any case; standard observational-coding diagnostics are. These include code-level distributional shape, between-dyad variance per code, code redundancy, and coding visibility.

The "net safety" framing derives from the parent trial's polyvagal-informed theoretical model (Porges, 2022). The parent study is explicit that the specific neurophysiological claims of that tradition have been contested (Grossman, 2023; Grossman et al., 2026; for a reply see Porges, 2026), and that it therefore treats the NSI codes "as behavioural heuristics that remain compatible with broader perceived-safety models, rather than as direct neuroanatomical claims" (de Blauwe, 2026, Appendix V). This paper adopts the same stance and evaluates the instrument as an observation sheet, inheriting the construct name rather than re-litigating its theoretical foundation. Full code definitions, thresholds, and scoring are given in Appendix A.

Two distinct failure sources are worth separating here. The first is architectural: the coding scheme mixes high-base-rate cooperative codes with low-base-rate distress codes within a scaffolded task, which the Discussion argues is sufficient on its own to produce the observed saturation-and-floor pattern regardless of the underlying theoretical model. The second is theoretical: if the polyvagal-informed safety construct the NSI operationalises is itself contested, some portion of the instrument's poor performance could reflect a weak construct rather than a purely structural coding problem. The present analysis is diagnostic of the first source only; it does not adjudicate the second, and a reader should not take the code-level pathology reported here as evidence either for or against the contested theoretical framework.

2.4 Code Architecture and Theoretical Maxima

Ten codes were tallied across seven bins spaced at two-minute intervals, with the first 30 seconds of each interval coded. Bins 5-7, the high-vulnerability final phase following the most personal disclosure prompts, were weighted at 1.5 in the engagement composite, reflecting the increasing-vulnerability gradient built into the FFP (Aron et al., 1997). Five codes were engagement-oriented: mutual eye contact sustained beyond three seconds (3EC), affirmative nodding (3ND), forward lean (FL), shared laughter (1SL), and warm tone (WT). Five were withdrawal-oriented: gaze aversion of three seconds or more (3GA), backward lean (BL), selftouch or fidget (ST), silence of five seconds or more (S5), and monologue by a single speaker of thirty seconds or more (MO).

Per-code theoretical maxima follow directly from the deposited scoring protocol (de Blauwe, 2026, Appendix D) rather than being free parameters. The engagement composite is computed per bin as (3EC + 3ND + FL + 1SL) -(3GA + BL + ST), with bins 5-7 multiplied by 1.5, so any single code entering that composite has a maximum absolute contribution of 4 unweighted bins plus 3 bins at 1.5, that is 8.5: this applies to all seven codes in the engagement composite, withdrawal codes included. The relational composite is computed per bin as (warmtone count) -(S5 + MO) and carries no vulnerability weighting, so warm tone, silence, and monologue each have a maximum of 7.0 across the seven bins. The composite is thus bounded at +41 (all four engagement codes present in every bin, no withdrawal codes, warm tone in every bin) and at -39.5, not at 0-42 as stated in earlier drafts.

Correction. Earlier drafts of this manuscript used maxima of 6.0 for self-touch, 2.0 for silence, and 1.0 for monologue. Those are the observed maxima in this dataset rather than the protocol-derived theoretical maxima, and using them inflates the percent-of-maximum figure for exactly the codes where the resulting denominator is smallest: the values are corrected here to 8.5, 7.0, and 7.0 respectively. The correction moves silence from 25.0% to 7.1% of maximum, reclassifying it as floored, and self-touch from 28.9% to 20.4%. It strengthens rather than weakens the diagnostic finding, and we flag it because a reader consulting the deposited protocol would otherwise find the discrepancy unexplained.

The asymmetry between 8.5 and 7.0 is not a defect: the codes are not on a common scale because the composites they enter are not weighted alike. For comparability we report per-code means as percentages of each code's own protocol maximum. One caveat follows from the deposited totals. Three dyads carry a silence total of 1.5 and one a warm-tone total of 3.5: halfpoint values that unweighted counts cannot produce, which suggests that the 1.5 weighting may also have been applied to some relational bins. Supplementary Table S1 therefore rescores the relational codes as if bin-weighted (maximum 8.5): warm tone moves from 89.0% to 73.3% of maximum and silence from 7.1% to 5.9%, and no code changes class. Supplementary Table S1b repeats the Table 3 simulation under that rescoring: the all-code and excl. MO+S5 rules are unchanged at every pilot size, and the excl. MO rule changes by at most 1.8 points.

2.5 Coding Protocol, Positionality, and What They Cannot Do

A single researcher coded all 15 dyads in real time using a paper coding sheet anchored to a stopwatch. The researcher was not blind to condition, and no second rater was used; the dyadby-code totals derived from the coding sheets are deposited at OSF under CC BY 4.0. Such a protocol cannot yield inter-rater reliability statistics, cannot rule out coder leniency as a source of absolute-level bias, and cannot support sequential analysis with confidence-interval estimation.

The argument advanced below is therefore distributional and resampling-based, rather than a claim about the absolute accuracy of any individual count.

Positionality. The lead author had prior training in Nonviolent Communication and in mindfulness practice, both directly relevant to the study context and, more specifically, to which behaviours were most readily counted and which were read as protective rather than pathological.

A second analyst who lacked that background and remained blind to the condition contributed to the companion qualitative analyses reported separately (de Blauwe et al., 2026), but for the present paper, the key limitation is unchanged, in that the NSI tally itself was completed by a single non-blinded coder in real time. Within-dyad non-independence (Kenny et al., 2006) was not modelled: all analyses treat the dyad as the unit of observation, and the non-independence of partners is acknowledged as a structural limitation of the feasibility-sample design.

3. Code-Level Diagnostic

>Code-Level Diagnostic

3.1 The Summary Tally Looks Healthy

Across 15 dyads the NSI summary score ranged from 9.0 to 37.5 on a composite bounded at -39.5 and +41 (M = 26.63, SD = 9.64, Mdn = 29.0; skewness = -0.72, kurtosis = -0.77). Both shape statistics fall within the conventional ±1 thresholds for approximate normality (Cramer & Howitt, 2004), and the spread was adequate, with no obvious floor or ceiling in the composite and no severe skewness. Taken at face value, the instrument appeared behaviourally sensitive.

The condition comparison on the summary tally was null (Mann-Whitney U = 24.5, p = .728, rrb = -.12), as was the comparison on the engagement component (U = 22.5, p = .561, rrb = -.20). The relational component showed a medium but non-significant trend (U = 39.5, p = .150, rrb = +.41), strengthening to U = 36.5, p = .086, rrb = +.52 in the deposited sensitivity analysis excluding the single dyad with an incomplete indicator (D04, whose shared-laughter cell was left blank; de Blauwe, 2026, Appendices E and I). Throughout, U is reported for the NVC arm and rrb = 2U/(n1n2) -1, so a positive value means that NVC dyads ranked higher. These are reported here solely because they are properties of this instrument rather than of the trial's primary outcome: a healthy-looking distribution and a null comparison together are exactly the combination that would ordinarily be read as "the instrument worked; the intervention did not." Section 3.2 shows why that reading would have been wrong.

Correction. Earlier drafts reported this contrast as U = 23.5, p = .64: that value was computed from a working spreadsheet in which the condition label was inverted for six of the fifteen dyads relative to the deposited allocation record, and the data file first posted to OSF carried the same working labels. That file has been replaced, and the six relabelled dyads are listed in condition_label_correction_log.csv. Two independent checks identify the deposited record as correct: the deposited appendix states that veto-card use was "0 across all NVC dyads and 2 across Mindfulness dyads," which the working spreadsheet contradicts, and the deposited labels reproduce every median, interquartile range, and test statistic in the deposited descriptive tables to the decimal, while the working labels reproduce none of them. The corrected values are reported above. The correction does not alter the substantive conclusion, in that the contrast remains null, but it matters because the direction differs: on the deposited labels the mindfulness arm scored higher on the composite (Mdn = 32.5 versus 27.0), the opposite of what the working labels implied.

3.2 Code-Level Inspection Reveals a Bimodal Architecture

Per-code descriptive statistics showed a sharply uneven distribution beneath the healthy summary score (Table 2; the contrast is shown directly in Figure 1). Four engagement codes operated at or near code maximum: affirmative nodding (93.7% of maximum, 60% of dyads at ceiling), warm tone (89.0%, 73% at ceiling), mutual eye contact (85.9%, 60% at ceiling), and forward lean (73.7%, 60% at ceiling). Three withdrawal codes were floor-bound: monologue (0.0%, never observed in any dyad), silence (7.1%), and backward lean (7.8%, observed in only 3 of 15 dyads). Only three codes produced usable between-dyad variance, spanning 18.8% to 39.6% of maximum. 4).

Figure 1 makes the measurement contradiction visible: the NSI composite appeared to have adequate spread across dyads, whilst inspection of its component codes showed a sharply uneven architecture, with four codes beyond the saturation threshold, three below the floor threshold, and only three codes retaining meaningful between-dyad variation. Note. Panel A shows the NSI summary tally across 15 dyads (range 9.0-37.5), binned in three-point intervals; apparent spread is adequate at the summary level. Panel B shows each code's mean as a percentage of its protocol-derived maximum, with whiskers at ±1 SD; dashed lines mark the pre-specified floor (15%) and saturation (70%) thresholds, both of which are exploratory first-formulation values recalibrated in Section 5.

Four engagement codes were saturated, and three withdrawal codes were floor-bound (identities and values in Table 2); the open marker denotes monologue's degeneracy (zero observations, zero variance). The panel contrast illustrates the central diagnostic: summary-level distributional health does not establish sensitivity to component codes. Generated by code/what_the_tally_hid.R from data/nsi_dyad_code_matrix.csv and data/nsi_codebook.csv, both deposited at OSF.

The mechanism matters. The NSI behaved as two instruments at once: one set of codes recorded near-universal cooperative surface behaviour, whilst another registered almost no visible withdrawal, and the two partially cancel in summation rather than producing a single coherent signal. Only three of ten codes carried usable between-dyad variance, and the largest of those, shared laughter, was itself observed in only 11 of 15 dyads.

Two features of Table 2 do most of the work in Section 5 and are worth flagging now.

First, five codes sit within the 10-point fragility band of a flagging threshold: forward lean is only 3.7 points above the saturation cut-point, gaze aversion and self-touch are 3.8 and 5.4 points above the floor cut-point, and backward lean and silence are 7.2 and 7.9 points below it, so their status is decided by a small number of observations. Second, one code is fully degenerate: the monologue was recorded 0 times across 105 coded bins across 15 dyads, yielding zero variance and a zero mean.

Companion qualitative work (de Blauwe et al., 2026) is consistent with the possibility that withdrawal-relevant experience was present but poorly captured by the observational codes; however, it neither validates the NSI nor establishes a mechanism.

4. Why the Composite Misled

>Why the Composite Misled

4.1 Engagement Codes Captured Baseline Cooperation

Bakeman and Quera (2011) are clear that high-base-rate cooperative behaviours (sustained eye contact, nodding, forward lean, warm tone) occur in most cooperative dyadic interactions in a quiet laboratory setting, so coding their frequency in a structured engagement task is close to coding whether the interaction proceeded cooperatively at all. A task built to generate reciprocal engagement will inflate high-base-rate engagement behaviours: if an observation sheet codes those same behaviours as the primary signal of relational safety, saturation is a predictable outcome rather than a surprise. That all four such codes here exceeded 73% of maximum, and that nodding did so with a standard deviation of 0.72 on a scale spanning 8.5 points, is consistent with this account.

4.2 Withdrawal Codes Targeted Overt Distress, Not Calm Withdrawal Beidel et al. (2010) and Leigh and Clark (2018) anchor backward lean, prolonged silence, and monologue in overt distress markers. Yet the construct of interest, calm withdrawal or masked relational distance, is subtler: it can be experienced within the interaction without producing the large, visible behaviours the NSI was designed to tally. The flooring of monologue, silence, and backwards lean, therefore, does not imply an absence of withdrawal, but indicates that the selected withdrawal codes were poorly matched to the construct's visibility profile in this task. The parent study reaches the same conclusion from the qualitative side, describing a "masking paradox" in which "a participant may appear behaviourally engaged whilst inwardly performing connection rather than experiencing it, and the NSI cannot reliably distinguish the two" (de Blauwe, 2026, Appendix V). This finding motivated a subsequent pivot toward a partner-rated alternative instrument, cited here only as a design response to the NSI's limitations: its construct development is reported separately (de Blauwe et al., 2026).

4.3 Task Scaffolding and the Observer Effect Compressed the Signal

Sprecher's (2021) review confirms that the FFP mandates turn-taking, reciprocal disclosure, and a shared script, scaffolding that introduces severe range restriction when the dependent variable is the frequency of overt engagement behaviours. This compression is exacerbated by the observer effect (McCambridge et al., 2014): the presence of a tallying researcher motivates polite engagement and suppresses visible awkwardness. The scaffolding account also gives the most direct explanation of the fully degenerate monologue code, in that a protocol which supplies each dyad with a fixed prompt list and mandates alternating turns makes an uninterrupted thirty-second single-speaker stretch close to procedurally impossible. The code was not measuring a rare behaviour: it was measuring a behaviour the task had excluded by design.

The null condition comparison in Section 3.1 is therefore multiply determined: no detectable intervention effect, signal compression by the task scaffold, and inflated cooperation due to observer presence are all consistent with it, and the summary tally cannot distinguish between them.

The design lesson is direct: in engagement-scaffolded tasks, code dimensions that can still vary when the scaffolding succeeds (response latency, dyadic repair, subtle asymmetry) rather than counting the engagement the task was designed to elicit (Hutchby & Wooffitt, 2008). When relational safety itself is the target, observational tallies should give way to partner-rated instruments that bypass the performative surface.

4.4 Single-Coder Live Observation Cannot Self-Detect This Pattern

A natural concern is that the NSI pattern reflects single-coder bias, but a unitary leniencyor-strictness account cannot explain codes moving simultaneously toward both the ceiling and the floor. One residual ambiguity, whether monologue's zero count reflects a true zero or coder inattention, is treated as the paper's most consequential limitation in Section 7.6, where it is addressed alongside the broader single-coder reliability constraint.

4.5 Why a Simpler Statistical Fix Does Not Resolve This

Readers may reasonably ask why the problem is not handled by a routine statistical remedy rather than a redesigned screening protocol. Box 1 states each candidate remedy and why it does not apply here. The short answer is that every remedy listed operates on the distribution of the composite, whereas the defect is the absence of between-dyad variance in the components, and no operation on a total can restore variance its parts never carried.

Box 1. Six remedies that do not fix a code-level saturation and floor problem.

Candidate remedy Why it does not resolve the defect

1 Use a robust statistic: median, trimmed mean, rank test

These address outliers and non-normality in the composite, and the composite here is not misbehaving: M = 26.63, SD = 9.64, spread across most of its range. Substituting a robust estimator for the mean of a total that already looks healthy changes nothing about the fact that monologue was recorded zero times in 105 coded bins.

2 Transform or rescale the composite

A monotone transformation is a relabelling of the same ordering: it cannot separate two dyads that received identical scores on a saturated code, and it cannot give a zerovariance code a variance.

3 Drop the pathological codes and re-score

This is diagnostically correct, in that it is what Section 5.3 does when it re-runs the screen with the degenerate and lowest-variance codes excluded, and what the revised protocol requires, but it is a diagnosis rather than a repair. Dropping four saturated and three floored codes leaves a three-code sheet whose content coverage was never established and whose largest contributing code, shared laughter, was observed in only 11 of 15 dyads. The composite already entered the parent record: it cannot be retrospectively re-specified.

4 Weight codes by variance, or model a latent factor

Variance weighting and latent-variable estimation both require between-dyad variance to estimate from. A degenerate code contributes no covariance at all, and with ten codes and fifteen dyads the covariance matrix is in any case not estimable.

5 Compute an internal-consistency coefficient

This is the remedy most likely to be proposed and the most misleading: standard software drops zero-variance items before computing alpha, so the single most pathological code is silently removed from the very diagnostic meant to detect it, and the coefficient is then reported on the nine codes that remain.

6

Recruit more dyads Saturation is a property of the code-task pairing rather than of the sample. A larger N estimates a ceiling-bound code's mean more precisely; it does not move that mean away from the ceiling. Section 5 shows this directly: detection is 93.6% at N = 15 and 90.8% at N = 4 once the degenerate and lowest-variance codes are excluded; in between it is flat and non-monotonic, with its minimum (87.0%) at N = 6.

5. Monte Carlo Screening Simulation

>Monte Carlo Screening Simulation

5.1 Motivation and Research Question

Heuristic 1, as originally formulated in this programme, asserts that code-level saturation and floor pathology "can be detected at pilot scale (N = 4-8 dyads)," whilst explicitly flagging this as a first-formulation value requiring deliberate replication. The assertion was never tested: it was an extrapolation from the fact that the pathology was visible in the full 15-dyad sample, rather than a demonstration that a smaller pilot subsample would reliably reveal it. This section reports a direct, falsifiable test.

Research question.

At what pilot sample size does a researcher screening a new liveobservation sheet using the procedure specified in Heuristic 1, namely: flag any code with a mean at or above 70% of its maximum as saturated, flag any code with a mean at or below 15% as floored, and conclude the instrument is at risk if at least one code is flagged saturated and at least one is flagged floored, have an acceptable probability of correctly reaching that conclusion, given that the true underlying pattern is the one observed in the full sample?

5.2 Method

The simulation resamples with replacement from the real per-dyad, per-code totals deposited at OSF; it does not generate, interpolate, or invent any dyad or participant characteristics, closeness scores, or trial outcomes at any point. For each of seven candidate pilot sizes (N = 2, 4, 6, 8, 10, 12, and 15 dyads), 20,000 independent resamples were drawn without stratification by condition, since the diagnostic question concerns instrument-level pathology rather than the between-condition comparison reported elsewhere. For each resample, per-code means were computed as a percentage of each code's protocol-derived theoretical maximum (Section 2.4), and the 70% saturation and 15% floor thresholds and the conjunctive detection rule specified in Heuristic 1 were applied, so that detection probability at each pilot size is the proportion of resamples in which the rule was satisfied .

Every simulation cell has its own seed, derived from the study's AsPredicted number (265934) and that cell's settings, so a cell that appears in two tables returns the same value in both. Monte Carlo uncertainty is reported as Wilson 95% intervals, which remain valid at 0% and 100%. The whole analysis is one base-R script (code/what_the_tally_hid.R), deposited at OSF; its core functions are reproduced in Appendix B.

Three nested code sets were evaluated, and terminology is used strictly throughout: degenerate means zero between-dyad variance, which in this dataset is true of monologue alone (SD = 0.00, never observed), whereas silence and backward lean are floored but not degenerate (SD = 0.76 and 1.73).

The primary analysis applies the rule to all ten codes exactly as originally specified, a first diagnostic variant removes the fully degenerate monologue code, and a second sensitivity variant additionally removes silence, the floored code with the smallest non-zero between-dyad variance and therefore the code nearest to degeneracy without being degenerate, in order to isolate how much of the primary result survives once near-invariant codes are set aside.

Such variants are not alternative specifications of the original heuristic. They are the instrument by which Section 5.5 identifies what the primary result actually depends on.

At N = 15, resampling with replacement from the full sample estimates baseline detection probability against sampling noise alone, since the best available estimate of the true pattern does contain both saturated and floored codes. Note. Detection probability is the share of 20,000 resamples per point flagged saturated (≥ 70% of code maximum) and floored (≤ 15%) under Heuristic 1, for N = 2-15. The shaded band marks the originally asserted N = 4-8 range; the dotted line marks an 80% reference, shown for orientation only and not as a valid rule here. MO = monologue (never observed); S5 = silence (mean 7.9 points below floor). The all-code curve is flat at 100.0% throughout: the never-observed monologue code always satisfies the floor condition, so this reflects degeneracy, not adequacy. Excluding monologue and silence gives a non-monotonic curve with its minimum at N = 6 (87.0%), not at N = 2. Ribbons are Wilson 95% Monte Carlo intervals. Values are those of

5.3 Results

Table 3, drawn by code/what_the_tally_hid.R.

Three results follow, and the first contradicts what was previously reported for this dataset.

Result 1: detection is certain at every pilot size, including the smallest. Under the rule exactly as originally specified, detection probability was 100.0% at every pilot size from N = 2 to N = 15; not even one of the 20,000 resamples at any size failed to flag both a saturated and a floored code. On its face, this vindicates the original N = 4-8 assertion and would justify relaxing it further. We argue in Section 5.5 that it does the opposite.

Result 2: The certainty is produced by degeneracy, not by adequacy. The mean number of codes flagged floored was 3.4-4.1 at every pilot size and did not increase with N, and Table 2 and Figure 1B show why: monologue has a mean and standard deviation of exactly zero, so its resampled mean is zero in every possible resample, and it is flagged floored with probability 1 regardless of N. The floor half of the conjunctive rule is not a test in this dataset: it is a constant.

Symmetrically, nodding sits 23.7 points above the saturation threshold, with SD = 0.72, and is flagged as saturated in essentially every resample, so the rule reduces to a tautology whose 100% detection rate carries no information about pilot adequacy.

Result 3: With monologue and silence removed, detection is flat and non-monotonic.

Excluding monologue alone leaves detection at 97.7-99.9%, whereas excluding both monologue and silence yields 90.8% at N = 4, 87.0% at N = 6, 91.7% at N = 8, and 93.6% at N = 15: a range of under 7 percentage points across a near-fourfold increase in pilot size, with the minimum at N = 6 rather than at the smallest size. The Monte Carlo intervals confirm that the dip is not noise: the N = 4 interval [90.3, 91.1] and the N = 6 interval [86.6, 87.5] do not overlap.

Detection in this design is simply not an increasing function of pilot size.

5.4 Sensitivity to Threshold Specification

5.5 Why Pilot Size Is Not the Operative Variable

The threshold-sensitivity results in Table 4 point to a mechanism that explains both the non-monotonicity and the failure of detection power to converge to 1. Consider backwards lean under a 10% floor threshold: its mean is 0.667, or 7.8% of its 8.5 maximum, the threshold in raw units is 0.85, and it is therefore flagged floored in the full sample. Yet its distribution across dyads is 12 zeros, one 1.5, one 2.0, and one 6.5, so flagging depends almost entirely on how many times the single high-leverage dyad enters the resample: drawing it twice in a resample of any size n ≤ 15 pushes the mean above 0.85 whenever 13/n > 0.85, which holds for all n ≤ 15.

Simulation confirms this reading. The probability that backward lean is flagged floored at a 10% threshold is 75.2% at N = 2, falls to 64.2% at N = 6, recovers to 72.5% at N = 10, and is 69.0% at N = 15, never approaching 1, whilst the probability of drawing the high-leverage dyad at least twice rises monotonically from 0.4% at N = 2 to 26.4% at N = 15 and 85.5% at N = 50 (exact binomial values). Increasing the pilot does not reduce the influence of the leverage observation: it makes encountering it more likely. Only at pilot sizes far beyond any feasible dyadic study (N ≈ 200, detection 93.7%) does the law of large numbers begin to dominate, and only because the true mean happens to fall on the floored side of the threshold at all.

Two general principles follow, and we state them as the paper's substantive contribution in place of the withdrawn sample-size recommendation.

Principle 1 (degeneracy dominates).

A code with zero observed variance is flagged with probability 1 at every pilot size. Any conjunctive screening rule containing such a code returns a positive result automatically, and the resulting "detection" is uninformative about whether the rest of the instrument was adequately screened. Degenerate codes must therefore be identified and set aside before detection power is assessed, rather than counted toward it.

Principle 2 (threshold proximity, not sample size, sets detection power).

For a code whose mean lies far from its flagging threshold relative to its between-unit dispersion, flagging is near-certain at any pilot size. For a code whose mean lies close to its threshold, flagging probability converges as N grows not to 1 but to a constant determined by the distribution of high-leverage observations, and may approach that constant non-monotonically. A screening protocol's detection power is therefore set by the least separated code that the rule depends on, and reporting a recommended pilot N without reporting per-code threshold distances conveys almost no information about whether the screen will work.

Neither principle is specific to the NSI, to the FFP, or to dyadic data. Both follow from the structure of threshold-flagging rules applied to resampled means, and both are directly testable on any deposited bin-level matrix. These two principles are consistent with, rather than independent of, the long-standing literature on floor and ceiling effects in classical test theory, which documents that scores compressed near a scale boundary lose discriminative power regardless of sample size (e.g., Cramer & Howitt, 2004). What the present analysis adds to that literature is not the existence of boundary compression, which is well established, but a codelevel decomposition showing that a composite built from oppositely-compressed components can mask the compression entirely, and a Monte Carlo demonstration that the resulting screening problem is a specificity problem rather than a power problem. The contribution is the diagnostic and calibration framework, not the discovery that boundaries reduce variance.

5.6 Specificity: Null-Condition Calibration of the Screening Rule

A detection-power estimate is uninterpretable without a known-null comparator: a 100% detection rate tells us the rule fires on this dataset, but it does not tell us whether the rule fires on any dataset. The screening rule was therefore calibrated against null conditions in which no saturation or floor pathology exists by construction, so that its false-positive rate can be read directly. Three nulls were examined.

Null 1: within-code permutation across dyads. The most natural-looking null, permuting each code's counts across dyads independently and thereby preserving every code's marginal distribution, is inert, and it is worth reporting why, because it is the null a reader is most likely to propose. Averaged over 200 permuted datasets (2,000 resamples each), detection was 100.0% at N = 4, 8, and 12: numerically identical to the unpermuted data. Permutation of this kind destroys the association between codes within a dyad, but the flagging rule does not use within-dyad association: it compares each code's resampled mean against a fixed threshold, and a permutation that preserves a code's marginal distribution preserves that mean exactly. Monologue remains zero in every permuted dataset, and nodding remains at 93.7% of the maximum. The property that makes this null appear conservative, preserved marginals, is precisely the property that makes it incapable of removing the pathology, since any null that leaves per-code marginals intact cannot test a per-code marginal rule.

Nulls 2 and 3: generative no-pathology populations. A valid null must relocate the codes relative to the thresholds, and two were constructed. In the homogeneous null every code's population mean was placed at 50% of its own protocol-derived maximum, midway between the 15% floor and 70% saturation cut-points, whereas in the heterogeneous null the ten codes were spread evenly across 25% to 65% of maximum, so that codes differ from one another but none is pathological. Both used a per-code dispersion of 0.20 × maximum, comparable to the dispersion of the three codes that actually carry variance in the real data (Table 2), with draws rounded to the 0.5 coding grid and truncated to the code's admissible range. Values are the proportion of 40,000 simulated pilots in which the conjunctive rule fired, that is, the false-positive rate. To test whether this calibration rests on two convenient null specifications, Table 6 crosses three dispersion levels with three mean placements at N = 4, 6, and 8 dyads, all under the conjunctive rule. The homogeneous and heterogeneous placements behave as the main analysis does: false positives fall with N and stay low at moderate dispersion. The third placement, which spreads the code means from 15% to 75% of maximum, is not a no-pathology null: its highest code sits above the 70% cut-point and its lowest is centred on the 15% cut-point, so the population already contains one saturated code and one floor-boundary code. It is reported as a boundary case, because it shows how quickly specificity collapses once any code's true mean lies at a threshold. 5 comparator exactly (9.15%, 4.01%, and 1.50%), because each cell has its own seed. Across the two genuine no-pathology placements, false positives fall with N and with lower dispersion; at N = 6 they range from 0.00% to 9.00%. The boundary-case placement stays at 36-49% at N = 6 because its outermost codes sit on or beyond a cut-point, which is why it is included. An earlier draft of this grid was computed under an either-extreme rule by mistake; that grid is retained as Supplementary Table S2 (Appendix C2). Note. Panel A places two quantities from the same rule on one scale: detection probability in the real data (100.0% at every N; Table 3) and the false-positive rate under the two no-pathology nulls (Table 5). Panel B rescales the false-positive curves; the dotted line marks 5%, and the shaded band marks the originally asserted N = 4-8. Heterogeneous null: 33.1% at N = 2, 9.2% at N = 4, 4.0% at N = 6, and 0.2% at N = 15 (error bars are Wilson 95% Monte Carlo intervals). Homogeneous null: 3.8% at N = 2 and essentially zero from N = 4. Taken together, the small-pilot risk here is a false alarm rather than a missed detection, so any minimum pilot size should derive from specificity rather than power. Drawn from Tables 3 and 5 by code/what_the_tally_hid.R; no separate simulation.

Result 4: specificity is high, and false positives are a small-pilot phenomenon. This calibration was run under the full ten-code rule, rather than only under the reduced-code variant as in earlier drafts. Under the homogeneous null, the rule fires in 3.84% of pilots at N = 2 and essentially never from N = 4 (0.03%). Under the more demanding heterogeneous null, the falsepositive rate is 33.1% at N = 2 and 9.2% at N = 4, then falls monotonically to 0.2% at N = 15.

The excl. MO and S5 rule gives lower but parallel rates (23.9%, 6.3%, and 0.2% at N = 2, 4, and 15), so the specificity finding is not an artefact of excluding monologue and silence: it holds for the rule exactly as originally specified. This reverses the pattern previously assumed. The risk at small pilot size is not that the screen misses real pathology, which it does not at any N tested, but that it occasionally manufactures pathology, because with two to four dyads a single atypical dyad can drag a mid-scale code across a cut-point. Note. False-positive rate under the heterogeneous null for the all-code rule and the excl. MO and S5 rule, N = 2-15, drawn from Table 5 by code/what_the_tally_hid.R. Both fall sharply between N = 2 and N = 6. The reduced-code rate is lower at every size but follows the same course, so the two rules lead to the same specificity conclusion. The dotted line marks 5%.

This yields the one defensible sample-size statement in the paper, and it is a specificity statement rather than a power statement: a code-level screen run on fewer than six dyads should not be trusted to distinguish genuine saturation-or-floor pathology from sampling noise, because its false-positive rate under a plausible no-pathology null exceeds 5% (9.2% at N = 4), whereas from six dyads upward it is 4.0% or less and continues to decline. Note that this reinstates a minimum pilot size on entirely different grounds than the N = 12 figure withdrawn in Section 5.8: that figure was offered as the sample size needed to achieve adequate power to detect, and it had not been generated by any executed simulation. The present figure is the sample size below which the screen's specificity becomes unreliable, and it is derived from a stated null.

Falsification criterion for the calibration itself.

The claim that the screening rule is validly calibrated, as opposed to merely firing on this dataset, would be falsified if, under a nopathology null of the kind specified above, the rule's false-positive rate (a) failed to fall below 5% by N = 6, or (b) failed to decline monotonically with increasing N. Both conditions were tested and neither was met: the rate is 4.0% at N = 6 and declines monotonically from N = 2 to N = 15 in both nulls. Had either condition been met, the detection-power curve in Table 3 would have to be read as an artefact of the resampling procedure rather than as evidence about the instrument, and Heuristic 1 would have to be withdrawn rather than revised. Crucially, this criterion is a calibration test of the procedure, whereas Criterion 1 in Section 5.7 concerns whether the pattern replicates in independent samples: the two are independent, and a methods claim of this type needs both.

5.7 Pre-Specified Falsification Criteria

Three falsification criteria are stated explicitly in Sections 5.6 and 5.7: one for the structural diagnostic, one for the simulation's two principles, and one for the calibration of the screening procedure itself, the last of which is evaluated in Section 5.6, since it is tested there. All three are restated compactly in Section 7.7 so that a reader encountering the Limitations first does not have to infer them.

Falsification criterion 1 (the diagnostic).

The four corrective heuristics, and the underlying claim that code-level saturation-and-floor pathology is a structural feature of engagement-scaffolded dyadic observation rather than an artefact of this instrument, would be undermined if a comparable Fast Friends-style dyadic sample produced a code-level distribution in which no code operated above the 70% saturation threshold and no code operated below the 15% floor threshold. Observing such a null pattern in an independent replication sample would falsify the structural claim.

Evaluation. This criterion was not met in the present data: the full-sample pattern in Table 2 shows four codes clearly saturated and three clearly floored, and every resample at every pilot size retained codes at both extremes, so the diagnostic survives this test. Because the test was applied within the same dataset from which the pattern was originally observed, however, it constitutes confirmation of internal consistency rather than independent replication. An equivalent test on a wholly independent Fast Friends-coded sample remains the appropriate external falsification opportunity.

Falsification criterion 2 (the simulation), as revised. This criterion was previously stated in terms of the detection-power curve: the claim would be falsified if an independent dataset showed detection reaching 80% at N ≤ 8. That formulation is now void, because the present data show detection reaching 100% at N = 2, and stating a criterion the data have already met in the opposite direction would be vacuous. The criterion is therefore restated to target the two principles that replace the curve.

Principles 1 and 2 in Section 5.5 would be falsified by an independently collected binlevel dataset in which (a) a code with zero observed variance was not flagged with probability approaching 1 under a threshold rule, which would indicate an error in the account of degeneracy, or (b) detection probability for a boundary-adjacent code (mean within 10 percentage points of its threshold) rose monotonically toward 1 with pilot size, which would indicate that the leverageobservation mechanism described in Section 5.5 is specific to this dataset's dispersion structure rather than general. Condition (b) is the substantive test, and we regard it as the more likely of the two to be met by some datasets. We specify in advance that meeting it would require Principle 2 to be restricted rather than merely qualified, specifically, restricted to distributions in which a small number of observations account for a large share of a code's total count, as is the case for backwards lean here, one dyad of fifteen contributing 65% of all observed instances.

Evaluation. Criterion 2 cannot be evaluated within the present dataset by construction: it is stated as the explicit condition under which the paper's central claim should be withdrawn.

5.8 Correction Record

A methods paper about component-level failure that goes undetected because nobody inspected the components has an obligation to report its own equivalent. Reproducibility checks against the deposited protocol, allocation record, and dyad-by-code matrix identified discrepancies in earlier analyses of this dataset, and one changes the scientific conclusion and is therefore reported here in full: the detection-power curve reported earlier rose from 41.2% at N = 4 to 88.4% at N = 15, and the paper's central conclusion, a 32-59% miss rate across N = 4-8 and a required 12-dyad minimum, was drawn from those values. Those numbers were not generated by the deposited script and are contradicted by it, and three mutually incompatible versions of the curve were in circulation, which is itself evidence that no simulation had been run. They are withdrawn and replaced by Table 3, and the direction of the finding reverses.

Five further discrepancies affected reported values without altering the argument: observed maxima used in place of protocol-derived maxima for three codes (Section 2.4), inverted condition labels for six of fifteen dyads (Section 3.1), incorrect composite bounds (Section 2.4), a reversed sign on the relational rank-biserial correlations (Section 3.1), and a null sensitivity grid computed under an either-extreme rule rather than the conjunctive rule it was described as testing (Table 6). A subsequent verification of every reference entry against Crossref and, where no DOI existed, against the primary source identified four entries that misidentified the work cited, and these are corrected. All analyses were recomputed from the deposited source records using the deposited R script, and a complete reconciliation log, covering superseded values, the affected quantities, the reference corrections, and the verification outputs, is retained in Appendix C and the project repository.

The relevant methodological point is not the errors themselves but what made them findable. Each was detectable only because the parent study deposited its scoring protocol, its allocation record, and its dyad-level composite table, and because the dyad-by-code matrix was deposited so the script could actually be run. Such transparency is the strongest available argument for Heuristic 4, and we would rather make it with our own errors than hypothetically.

5.9 A Portable Margin Index

Sections 5.3 to 5.6 needed the raw dyad-by-code matrix and a simulation. Most teams screening a new sheet will have neither at the planning stage: they will have a small pilot table, or a published table of code means and standard deviations. We therefore asked whether the simulation's results could be recovered from those summary statistics alone. If they can, the screen transfers beyond this dataset without anyone having to rerun our code.

For each code, let p be its mean as a proportion of its protocol maximum, s its betweenunit standard deviation on the same scale, and d the distance from p to the cut that decides its status: 70% for a saturated code, 15% for a floored code, and the nearer of the two for a code with variance. In a pilot of N units the code's mean has a standard error of about s/√N, so its margin in standard-error units is z = d√N/s. The probability that the pilot gives the code the same status as the full data is then approximately Φ(z) for a saturated or floored code, and Φ((0.70p)√N/s) -Φ((0.15 -p)√N/s) for a code with variance, where Φ is the standard normal distribution function. Treating codes as independent, the probability that the conjunctive rule fires is the probability of at least one saturated code multiplied by the probability of at least one floored code. The same expression, given the centres and spread of a no-pathology population, returns a false-alarm rate. Nothing in it is specific to the NSI: it needs a mean, a standard deviation and a pilot size for each code.

We tested the index against the simulation on three targets (Table 9, Figure 5; the full grid is Table S5 at OSF). First, code-level stability. Across the nine non-degenerate codes and seven pilot sizes (63 points), the index tracked the simulated proportion of pilots that reproduced each code's status (r = .97). From N = 4 upwards the mean absolute error was 1.2 percentage points and 96% of predictions fell within 5 points; the largest error was 8.3 points, for Forward lean at N = 4. At N = 2 the mean error rose to 7.3 points, as expected when a pilot mean rests on two units.

Second, sheet-level detection under the rule excluding MO and S5. From N = 4 the index matched Table 3 to within 1.8 points on average, but it did not reproduce the dip at N = 6 (87.0% simulated against 91.7% predicted). That dip is the single-dyad leverage effect described in Section 5.5, which a smooth formula cannot see. Third, false alarms under the heterogeneous null. The index over-predicted the simulated rate at every size from N = 4 (12.7% against 9.2% at N = 4; 6.2% against 4.0% at N = 6), and so placed the 5% specificity floor at N = 8 rather than N = 6. Because codes in the null are independent by construction, this gap comes from the rounding and truncation of simulated scores to the coding grid, which the formula ignores. Its error therefore runs in the safe direction: a team planning from the index alone will pilot slightly more units than needed, not fewer.

Table 9 shows what the index gives a team at the planning stage: for each code, the pilot size at which its status would be reproduced in 95% of pilots. Codes far from a cut need very few units. Affirmative nodding needs 2, Warm tone 4, Mutual eye contact 5, and Silence 6, and in each case the simulation agreed to within one simulated pilot size. Codes close to a cut need far more. Self-touch would need about 48 units and Gaze aversion about 173, and Forward lean, 3.7 points above the saturation cut with an SD of 39 points, would not stabilise within 200. This is Principle 2 expressed as a number: a fragile code is one whose required pilot is larger than any pilot a team could run, and the right response is to redesign the code rather than to enlarge the pilot. The index also marks where it should not be trusted. One dyad supplies 65% of Backward lean's total, so the function raises a leverage warning. The formula asks for 22 units, but, as Section 5.5 showed, the simulated stability of this code does not rise steadily with N.

The index is implemented as screen_sheet() in tool/screen_sheet.R at OSF, a base-R function that takes any unit-by-code matrix and the codes' protocol maxima and returns Table 9 for that sheet, together with the predicted probability that a pilot of a chosen size would flag it. A template in the same folder shows the expected layout, and where only a published table of means and standard deviations is available, margin_index() in the same file runs the calculation from those figures alone. The main script calls the same function, so every value in this section is part of the reproduction check (Section 8). Two limits apply. The index assumes that a pilot mean is roughly normally distributed, which fails for very small pilots and for codes dominated by one or two units, and the function warns in the second case. And although the formula is general, its accuracy was checked here on one sheet and one family of null populations, so it is offered as a planning aid, to be checked against a simulation whenever the raw data allow.

A related, exploratory question is what the composite's spread is actually made of (Supplementary Material S6). Contrary to our expectation, the codes classed as saturated or floored are not silent: together they supply 65.9% of the variance of the NSI total, and Forward lean alone supplies 21.7%, the largest share of any code. Yet that spread is concentrated in very few dyads. Within each such code, 64.6-87.3% of the squared deviation comes from the three to six dyads that leave the bound, so it records how often a few dyads fall off a ceiling or rise off a floor rather than graded differences between dyads. Summed separately, the three varying codes and the seven stuck codes each track the total (ρ = .83), while agreeing with each other only weakly (ρ = .43, 95% CI [-.10, .77]), and one dyad moves ten rank places between the two orderings. A tally that looks healthy can therefore be the sum of two weakly related rankings. Note. Panel A: for each non-degenerate code and pilot size, the proportion of 20,000 resampled pilots that gave the code its full-sample status, In scaffolded tasks, code what can still vary, and check that the task has not excluded a code by design. When task scaffolding and observer presence produce polite engagement, frequency-of-engagement codes are likely to saturate because they track behaviours the task is designed to elicit (McCambridge et al., 2014). Code instead of latency, repair, asymmetry, depth beyond prompt, or responsiveness: dimensions that discriminate between dyads even when all dyads are cooperating at the scaffolded surface (Hutchby & Wooffitt, 2008). The extension follows from the degenerate monologue code: before piloting, ask of every withdrawal code whether the protocol's own turn-taking, prompting, or timing rules make the behaviour procedurally impossible. A code the task has excluded by construction will read as a floor effect, but it is in fact a specification error, and it will silently guarantee a positive result for any conjunctive screening rule that contains it.

Heuristic 4.

Deposit unit-level coding data. When single-coder observation is structurally necessary, open deposit of unit-level coding matrices (bin-level where possible) is the only retrospective error-detection mechanism available, and it is also the only mechanism that makes a detectionpower simulation like the one in Section 5 possible at all (Open Science Collaboration, 2015). In line with emerging reporting guidance (Hillen et al., 2025), raw coding matrices should be treated as standard research assets rather than optional supplements. Section 5.8 demonstrates the point with this manuscript's own corrections, including one that reversed the direction of its central claim.

6.3 The Screening Protocol

The Screening Protocol >Discussion and Limits

7.1 Two Findings, One Instrument

The two findings reported here are related but distinct. The first is diagnostic and specific to this instrument (Section 3). The second is the paper's primary methodological contribution and matters independently of the first: the pilot-size question around which the original heuristic was built is not the operative one (Section 5). The simulation indicates that detection under this threshold-based rule was governed by code degeneracy and proximity to the threshold rather than by pilot size alone (Section 5.5). The revised protocol accordingly requires per-code variance and threshold-distance reporting rather than a universal pilot-size recommendation.

7.2 Relationship to the Self-Report Floor and Ceiling Literature

Item-level floor and ceiling effects are well documented in self-report instruments: individual Barthel Index items, for example, can show pronounced floor effects concealed in the total score, and such problems can be diagnosed retrospectively via item-response modelling and corrected in subsequent administrations (Hung et al., 2018; Loewen & Anderson, 1988).

Live observational coding offers no comparable route to retrospective correction: there is no second administration and no way to re-ask participants. The heuristics offered here are therefore a predata-collection screening protocol rather than a post hoc diagnostic, and that is precisely why the detection question is not academic. If the only opportunity to catch the pathology is at pilot stage, then what determines whether that safeguard works is worth getting right.

It is worth being explicit about the limits of the analogy, since one might reasonably ask why the relevant comparison is psychometric scale validation rather than observational-coding reliability or generalisability theory. Generalisability theory would treat the codes as facets and estimate variance components, which requires multiple raters or occasions and is therefore unavailable here by construction. Sequential-analysis approaches (Bakeman & Quera, 2011) would address contingency structure rather than marginal distributional adequacy. The specific gap this paper addresses sits between these: the marginal, code-level adequacy of a frequencycoded instrument administered once by one observer, where no reliability facet exists to be modelled, and the only available leverage is the deposited count matrix itself.

7.3 Relationship to the Companion Manuscripts and the Parent Record

The parent trial's substantive findings, its closeness comparison, permutation sensitivity tests, and feasibility-progression scoring, are reported and interpreted in full in a companion manuscript and are not restated here (de Blauwe & Fidalgo, 2026a). That manuscript's methodological argument, that dyad-level missing data require dyad-level handling, is conceptually parallel to this paper's argument that detection claims require direct testing rather than informal extrapolation, but the two papers report entirely separate empirical content and neither reproduces the other's inferential results. A related qualitative methodological paper examines the same trial's written debrief corpus to develop a framework for partner-recognition risk in dyadic qualitative data disclosure (de Blauwe et al., 2026). Its themes and disclosuregovernance framework are unrelated to the observational-coding diagnostic reported here and are not discussed further.

The parent study's own deposited record (de Blauwe, 2026) already documents the ceiling effect at the composite level and already concludes that "the NSI in its current form lacked sensitivity to discriminate between conditions." We want to be clear that the present paper does not claim to have discovered that. Its contributions relative to that record are three: the decomposition of the effect to the code level with protocol-derived denominators, the demonstration that the composite's apparent distributional health is produced by the opposing failures rather than coexisting with them, and the resampling analysis of what determines detectability. Novelty should be assessed on those three things.

7.4 Generalisation Conditions

This case study motivates a testable risk hypothesis for similarly brief, structured, livecoded interactions, rather than establishing prevalence across interaction paradigms. The mechanism should be suspected wherever a coding scheme mixes high-base-rate cooperative behaviours with low-base-rate distress markers within a brief, structured task that scaffolds one side of that mixture and is coded in real time by a single observer, and it should be expected nowhere on the strength of a single coding sheet. Risk is plausibly lower in long-form interactions, in covert video coding that removes the researcher-in-the-room effect, and in schemes prioritising repair, latency, or sequential contingency over frequency counts.

Principles 1 and 2 (Section 5.5) carry further than the pathology, because they follow from the arithmetic of threshold rules rather than from any feature of dyadic observation. The specific numbers, the 70% and 15% thresholds and the 10-point fragility band, are values calibrated to this design, not universal cut-offs.

7.5 Implications for Practice

Researchers using bin-coded observation sheets in structured dyadic work should treat code-level distributional screening as a core pilot-stage check for live, threshold-based observation sheets, and should stop treating pilot size as the lever that makes it work. The primary diagnostic questions are "Does any code have zero variance?", "how far is each code from its flagging threshold?", "Which codes are actually carrying variance?", and "could the construct of interest manifest without producing any of the coded behaviours?" (West & Kenny, 2011), rather than "does the summary tally look normal?" and not "have I piloted enough dyads?" Aligning with SCOBe reporting guidance (Hillen et al., 2025), funders and publishers should treat bin-level data deposition and a per-code threshold-distance table as normative expectations for observational-coding studies. To our knowledge, no published FFP observational study has yet deposited bin-level coding sheets alongside code-level distributional analyses, and researchers with archived FFP bin-level data are invited to apply this diagnostic and this simulation to their own instruments.

7.6 Limitations

The obvious objection to this paper is that fifteen dyads and one non-blinded coder are enough to demonstrate an interesting dataset but not to establish a diagnostic framework, and in our view, that objection is correct. What is offered is a bounded case study with two analytically derived principles and a screening protocol, not a validated framework. The NSI tally was completed in real time by a single non-blinded coder. We cannot exclude coder-related explanations, including interpretation drift, expectation effects, or selective attention, although the observed pattern fits a code-visibility mismatch better than a single-direction scoring bias (Section 4.4). The resampling procedure inherits any coder-level bias present in the original counts and cannot distinguish instrument-level pathology from a hypothetical coder-level artefact.

The degenerate code is the most consequential limitation. The primary detection result, 100% at every pilot size under the original all-code conjunctive rule, is driven entirely by a code with zero observations across 105 coded bins. A true zero base rate produced by the turn-taking scaffold (Section 4.3) and a column that ceased to be attended to are observationally indistinguishable in the deposited sheets. The deposited protocol and coding sheet define this code (MO) as monologue by a single speaker exceeding thirty seconds; the 'Monotone' label appearing in one section of the parent study's appendices was a transcription inconsistency, not a distinct construct, confirmed via written correspondence with the coding-protocol author retained in the project repository (Appendix C4). Accordingly, the all-code detection rate is reported as a primary finding, and the MO + S5-excluded columns in Tables 3 and 4 are retained only as a robustness check. Nothing in the analysis depends on resolving whether the zero is genuine: if it is genuine, the code is a specification error the protocol should have caught (Heuristic 3, extended); if it is a coding lapse, the code is a data-quality failure. Either way, it must be excluded from the conjunctive rule, which is what Principle 1 requires. Yet a replication with a genuinely non-degenerate code set would be a stronger test of Principle 2 than this dataset can provide.

The Monte Carlo procedure treats the observed 15-dyad sample as the best available estimate of the true underlying code-level architecture and resamples from it with replacement.

With fifteen source units the resampled distribution is coarse, and the leverage mechanism identified in Section 5.5 is in part a property of that coarseness, though we note that the mechanism does not disappear as the resample size grows and would operate identically in a larger source sample with similarly concentrated counts. Detection-power estimates should be read as first-formulation values for a class of designs, not as a validated power table.

The null-condition calibration in Section 5.6 rests on generative populations rather than on real no-pathology data, because no independent Fast Friends-coded bin-level dataset is publicly available to serve as one. The false-positive rates in Table 5 are therefore conditional on the assumed per-code dispersion of 0.20 × maximum and on the assumption that a nonpathological instrument places its codes somewhere between 25% and 65% of maximum. A real instrument with markedly wider per-code dispersion would show a higher false-positive rate at every N, and the six-dyad specificity floor would move upward accordingly. The rank ordering, false positives concentrated at very small pilots and declining monotonically, is stable across the dispersion values and mean placements examined (Table 6), but the specific cut-point is not a constant of nature. Applying the same calibration to an independent instrument is the natural next step, and it requires only that its bin-level matrix be deposited.

Finally, the conjunctive rule tested is the one specified in the original Heuristic 1.

Alternative rules were not tested beyond the reduced-code variants reported in Table 3, with one exception: the either-extreme rule (flag on any single saturated or floored code) is reported for the null grid in Supplementary Table S2. A rule requiring two codes on the same side would be a direct extension.

7.6.1 Limitations Addressed Versus Not Addressed

Table 8 summarises each limitation raised above by whether it is addressed in this paper, partially addressed, or left open, along with a severity rating reflecting its potential impact on the paper's substantive conclusions (Principles 1 and 2 and the specificity floor). The three falsification criteria are restated compactly as this paper's refutable claims; a claim-boundary summary and an instrument-diagnostics reporting checklist are provided as Supplementary Material S3 and S4 at OSF. First, the structural diagnostic (Section 3) would be falsified by an independent Fast Friends-style sample showing no code above 70% and none below 15% of its protocol maximum. Second, the simulation's two principles (Section 5.5) would be falsified by an independent bin-level dataset in which a zero-variance code was not flagged with probability approaching 1, or in which detection probability for a boundary-adjacent code rose monotonically toward 1 with pilot size. Third, the calibration claim (Section 5.6) would be falsified if the screening rule's false-positive rate under a no-pathology null failed to fall below 5% by N = 6 or failed to decline monotonically in N: this criterion was tested and not met, and had it been met the detection-power curve would have to be treated as an artefact of the resampling procedure rather than as evidence about the instrument. The first two are stated in full in Section 5.7 and the third in Section 5.6. We regard the second as the more likely of the two to be revised by replication, and we would treat such a revision as the intended function of publishing the criterion rather than as a failure of the paper. Section 5.8 records that an earlier state of this manuscript, written before the simulation was actually run, made the opposite prediction, and that running it reversed the conclusion. We take that as an argument for the criterion-and-deposit approach rather than against it.

7.8 Practical Screening Algorithm Box 2. Screening a live-observation sheet for code-level pathology.

Practical Screening Algorithm Box 2. Screening a live-observation sheet for code-level pathology.

Step What to do, and why it matters

Before treating any pilot as evidence that an observation sheet is sound, work at the level of codes rather than totals, in this order.

1

Inspect code degeneracy first For every code, compute its between-dyad variance in the pilot data and identify any code with zero or near-zero variance. A code that never varies is not weak evidence about the instrument: it makes the conjunctive screening rule fire automatically and renders any detection-rate figure computed from that pilot uninterpretable as a sample-size guide. Report degenerate codes as a separate finding and re-run the screen with them excluded.

2

Report each code's distance from its threshold For every code, report its mean as a percentage of its own protocolderived maximum, never its observed maximum, together with the signed distance to whichever flagging threshold it is nearer. This distance, rather than the number of dyads, is the quantity that determines whether the pilot could have caught the problem: a code within a few percentage points of a cut-point will be decided by a single high-leverage dyad at any feasible N.

3 Run an explicit no-pathology calibration

Simulate the screening rule against a generative null population in which no code is saturated or floored by construction, and report the resulting false-positive rate at your actual pilot size. Do not use a within-code permutation null for a rule built on per-code marginals: it preserves those marginals and is therefore inert (Section 5.6).

4

Do not state or accept a pilot-N claim without steps 1-3

A recommended pilot size quoted without degeneracy inspection, threshold distances, and a calibrated false-positive rate is not a power statement: it is an unexamined assumption, and in this paper's own earlier drafts it was wrong in direction as well as magnitude. Where a minimum is needed, derive it from specificity, the smallest N at which the false-positive rate under a stated null is acceptable, and label it as such.

scored present (1) or absent (0) within each observation window, whilst warm tone is rated per bin as warm, neutral, or cold, and only warm ratings are summed into the composite. Silence immediately following a Veto Card activation is coded as neutral, not withdrawal.

A3. Time-sampling and bin structure.

The 14-minute interaction is divided into seven observation points at 0:00, 2:00, 4:00, 6:00, 8:00, 10:00, and 12:00, with the first 30 seconds of each coded. Bins 5-7 carry ×1.5 weighting in the engagement composite to reflect the elevated cost of maintaining engagement during deep-disclosure phases. The coding sheet carries adjacent Notes and Veto columns, so the veto and distress protocols were embedded in the instrument itself rather than described only narratively.

A4. Composite scoring and verification.

Engagement (per bin) = (3EC + 3ND + FL + 1SL) -(3GA + BL + ST), with bins 5-7 multiplied by 1.5. Relational (per bin) = (warm-tone count) -(S5 + MO), unweighted. Total NSI = Σ Engagement + Σ Relational. Row-level verification confirms that both component identities and the total identity hold exactly for all 15 deposited rows, and that all fifteen dyad-level triples reproduce the parent study's deposited dyad-level table to the decimal (Appendix C). The maxima that follow from these formulae are 8.5 for every code entering the engagement composite and 7.0 for warm tone, silence, and monologue, and the composite is bounded at -39.5 and +41. (f) Rank-biserial sign. Earlier drafts gave the relational contrasts as rrb = -.41 and -.52. With U reported for the NVC arm and rrb = 2U/(n1n2) -1, both are positive (+.41 and +.52): NVC dyads ranked higher on the relational component.

A5. A procedural note relevant to Section 4.

(g) Null sensitivity grid. The earlier Table 6 grid was computed under an either-extreme rule (flag if any code is saturated or floored), not the conjunctive rule it was described as testing. Table 6 now uses the conjunctive rule; the either-extreme grid is retained as Supplementary Table S2.

(h) Simulation settings. The deposited script draws 20,000 resamples per cell for Tables 3 and 4 and 40,000 simulated pilots per cell for the null models in Tables 5 and 6. N = 2 is now included in Table 3, and the tables after Table 6 have been renumbered.

(i) Registration statement. Earlier drafts stated that use of the NSI was pre-registered.

AsPredicted #265934 does not name the NSI; the statement is corrected in the Author Note and in Sections 2.2 and 8.

C3. Internal contradiction in the parent record.
