Two parent-appendix tables report the same composites on incompatible scales; recomputation matches one exactly. Flagged for resolution there: it affects nothing in this paper, which derives entirely from the dyad-by-code matrix.

C4. The tenth code.

One section of the parent study's appendices labels the tenth code "Monotone". The deposited protocol and coding sheet define it as monologue by a single speaker for 30 seconds or more (MO), and "Monotone" was a transcription inconsistency, not a distinct construct. This was confirmed in written correspondence with the coding-protocol author, retained in the project repository, and adjudicated by ARF. MO is analysed as monologue throughout.

6.1 What the Simulation Changes

The original Heuristic 1 asked the wrong question. "How many dyads do I need to pilot?" presupposes that detection power is an increasing function of sample size, which holds for parameter estimation but not for threshold flagging (Sections 5.3-5.5). Such an error carries a practical cost in both directions: a researcher who followed the original heuristic and piloted 12 dyads rather than 4 would have spent three times the resources for no gain in detection, while one who followed the withdrawn revision would have been told that a 4-dyad pilot carries a 59% miss rate when it carries none.

The corrective is not a bigger number but two reported quantities: each code's observed variance, to identify degeneracy, and its signed distance from each flagging threshold, to identify fragility. Both are computable from a pilot of any size, including N = 4, and both are already in

Table 2.6.2 The Four Corrective Heuristics, Revised These heuristics are testable hypotheses derived from a single dataset via both direct inspection and Monte Carlo resampling. The thresholds are first-formulation values, and replication on independently collected datasets should test whether they generalise or require recalibration.

What matters most is the logic of the screen, not the specific numbers.

Heuristic 1 (rewritten).

Report per-code threshold distances and variances, not a pilot sample size. Inspect every code for saturation (mean at or above 70% of that code's protocol maximum) and floor (mean at or below 15%) before main data collection, and report for each code its mean as a percentage of maximum, its between-unit standard deviation, the number of units in which it was observed at all, and its signed distance in percentage points to each threshold. Then apply three decision rules. (a) Any code with zero observed variance is a degenerate code: flag it, exclude it from the conjunctive rule, and treat it as a design fault requiring the code to be redefined or dropped, rather than as evidence that the screen has succeeded. (b) Any code whose mean lies within 10 percentage points of a threshold is a fragile code: its flagging status is unreliable at any feasible pilot size, and both the flagged and unflagged interpretations should be carried forward. (c) Do not assume that a larger pilot improves detection. Where a code's distribution is dominated by a few high-count units, detection probability converges to a constant below 1 and may decline with N (Section 5.5). We withdraw both the original N = 4-8 recommendation and the N = 12 recommendation that replaced it, and recommend no pilot-size figure at all. Pilot size should be set based on the study's estimation needs, not by this screen.

Heuristic 2.

Match code visibility to construct visibility. Overt-behaviour codes detect overt constructs. Subtler constructs, such as calm withdrawal, masked distress, and low-warmth compliance, may be present without producing the large, visible behaviours that frequency codes record (Michie et al., 2011). The screening question is whether the construct could plausibly manifest without any of the coded behaviours occurring. If yes, overt-frequency codes are the wrong instrument family, and partner-rated instruments or visibility-matched codes are more appropriate.

8. Open Materials and Reproducibility Pre-registration.

The parent trial was pre-registered on AsPredicted (#265934; https://aspredicted.org/ee44eu.pdf), but that registration did not name the NSI. This exploratory case study is not itself pre-registered: Section 2.2 sets out the full scope statement, and the preregistration checklist submitted with this article lists each point of divergence.

Data, materials, and code.

A fully documented reproducibility package is publicly available in the Open Science Framework (OSF) at https://osf.io/mjb6h, under a CC BY 4.0 licence. It contains the most detailed disclosure-screened materials that can be shared without compromising participant confidentiality: the de-identified dyad-by-code matrix for all 15 attending dyads (nsi_dyad_code_matrix.csv); the codebook with protocol-derived maxima, code polarities, and bin weighting (nsi_codebook.csv); a data dictionary; the condition-label correction log; one analysis script in R (what_the_tally_hid.R); every output table and figure, including Supplementary Tables S1, S1b, S2, S5, S6a, and S6b and Supplementary Figure S6; and the list of values reported in this article (expected_values.csv).

The tool/ folder holds screen_sheet.R, the margin-index function of Section 5.9, with a template matrix and maxima file so that it can be run on any sheet. The README provides a file-to-output map and step-by-step reproduction instructions. A single command, Rscript code/what_the_tally_hid.R, regenerates every table, figure, and in-text value in under a minute. number generator type, and derives a separate seed for every simulation cell from the AsPredicted number (265934). It then compares its own output with the 105 values reported in this article (expected_values.csv) and states how many were reproduced; the deposited run, made with R 4.5.2, reproduced all 105. The package also records the session information and SHA-256 checksums for every deposited file.

The R script supersedes the Python scripts and the R transcription deposited with earlier versions of this project, which are withdrawn. Appendix B reproduces its core functions. The public analytic inputs are disclosure-screened derivatives of the study records rather than synthetic replacements. Superseded values from earlier drafts are documented as withdrawn in Appendix C and in CHANGES.md, and are not used in any analysis reported here.

Raw participant-authored written responses, participant-and dyad-linkage information, re-identification keys, scheduling records, coder-level source files, and the restricted internal coding-of-record are not openly shared. Removal of direct identifiers does not eliminate disclosure risk in dyadic research: participants may recognise their own encounter and infer their partner's identity from shared contextual detail. The public package therefore provides the maximum safely shareable analytic data, documentation, materials, code, and outputs. Where ethically and legally permissible, the responsible data controller may consider requests for access to restricted materials on a case-by-case basis, subject to disclosure-risk assessment, applicable ethics and data-sharing requirements, and safeguards including non-re-identification conditions.

An anonymised provenance record documenting data sources, collection procedures, processing, derivation of the analytic datasets, and author-specific roles in data and code handling is available at https://ascollected.org/VS9_QL8. The record contains no participant-level data, restricted source material, direct identifiers, or information intended to facilitate re-identification.

Reported statistics.

Every quantity reported here was recomputed from the deposited dyad-by-code matrix and crossvalidated against the parent study's deposited appendices. Appendix C reports that crossvalidation, including the corrections to this manuscript and one internal contradiction identified in the parent record.

Open Practices badges.

Open Data and Open Materials badges are claimed. The Preregistration badge is not claimed: the parent trial was pre-registered, but neither the NSI nor any analysis reported here was named in that registration. The NSI tracks moment-to-moment shifts in trauma-informed Fast Friends Procedure dyads through ten low-inference behavioural codes, prioritising listener behaviours on the principle that voluntary co-regulation cues from the non-speaking partner provide the cleanest available index of relational safety. The architecture draws on polyvagal theory (Porges, 2022) and on classical ethology (Tinbergen, 1952), the latter supplying the time-sampled, low-inference coding framework. Consistent with the parent study's own position, the codes are treated as behavioural heuristics compatible with broader perceived-safety models rather than as direct neuroanatomical claims (Grossman, 2023; Grossman et al., 2026). Following equipment failures and participant feedback that sensor attachment heightened anxiety, physiological instrumentation was removed under an approved ethics amendment (UEL 09012026 AGAF), and the study pivoted to non-invasive behavioural phenotyping.

A2. Code set.

Five engagement codes: mutual eye contact sustained beyond 3 s (3EC), affirmative nodding of three or more nods within the window (3ND), forward lean with torso beyond 90 degrees (FL), shared laughter mutual and beyond 1 s (1SL), and warm tone (WT). Five withdrawal codes: gaze aversion beyond 3 s looking down or away (3GA), backward lean with torso away (BL), self-touch or fidget at hands or neck beyond 2 s (ST), silence beyond 5 s with no turn-taking (S5), and monologue by a single speaker beyond 30 s (MO). Binary codes are Because only the first 30 seconds of each two-minute interval were coded, the observer had a fixed, brief, high-attention window per bin, and 105 coded bins in total across the study. This plausibly biases coding toward the most salient and most visible behaviours, which are, by construction, the cooperative ones. Such a mechanism is concrete procedural support for the code-visibility account in Section 4.2, and it is a design feature of the instrument rather than an execution failure. It is also relevant to the degenerate-code limitation in Section 7.6: a behaviour defined as exceeding 30 seconds is being screened for within a 30-second window.

A6. Instrument evolution.

Pilot deployment across three calibration dyads revealed a negativity artefact from withdrawalfirst column ordering, undocumented condition awareness, and insufficient distress guidance. The revision adopted engagement-first ordering, added a blinding checkbox, expanded the distress protocol, and consolidated session materials into a single form. Core codes, bin structure, and scoring algorithm were unchanged between versions.

Appendix B: Analysis Script (R)

The complete analysis is one base-R script, what_the_tally_hid.R, deposited at OSF with the data it reads. Run from the top folder of the package with Rscript code/what_the_tally_hid.R, it validates the inputs, regenerates every table and figure, and checks its output against the 105 values reported here. The listing below reproduces, abridged, the four core functions that implement the screen and both simulations; the full script is commented line by line, and a plainlanguage walkthrough (code/R_SCRIPT_WALKTHROUGH.md) accompanies it.

Cross-Validation Against the Deposited Parent Record

Every quantity here was recomputed from the deposited dyad-by-code matrix and checked against the parent study's appendices (de Blauwe, 2026), establishing that matrix as the authentic source layer beneath the parent's dyad-level reporting and logging the corrections summarised in Section 5.8.

C1. Independent quantities reproduced exactly. Without adjustment:

(a) all fifteen dyad-level engagement/relational/total triples, in row order; (b) both veto-card flags, same rows; (c) the two component identities and total identity of Appendix A4, every row; (d) all six condition medians and IQRs; (e) the relational contrast, U = 39.5, p = .150, rrb = +.41; (f) the sensitivity analysis excluding the dyad with an incomplete indicator, U = 36.5, p = .086, rrb = +.52, which is reproduced only when D04, the one dyad with a blank cell on its coding sheet, is excluded, confirming row alignment; and (g) composite descriptives, M = 26.63, SD = 9.64, Mdn = 29.0, range 9.0-37.5.

Items (d)-(f) reproduce only under the deposited condition labels: the basis for the Section 3.1 correction.

C2. Reconciliation log.

(a) Detection power. Earlier drafts showed 41.2% (N = 4) rising to 88.4% (N = 15), implying a 12-dyad minimum; the deposited script returns 100.0% at every N (Table 3). Withdrawn values are logged for lineage only and should not be cited.

Steps 4 to 8 with a short script: the deposited code (Section 8) implements Steps 3 to 8 and can be pointed at any unit-by-code count matrix. The order matters more than the tooling. Steps 1 and 2 must precede data collection, because a code whose maximum was mis-derived, or whose behaviour the task forbids, cannot be repaired afterwards, a point the case study demonstrates at its own expense (Section 5.8). Step 4 must precede Step 5, because a degenerate code silently satisfies a conjunctive rule and will make a broken instrument look detected, and Step 8 must precede any reassurance drawn from a negative screen. Step 9 is the protocol's main departure from common practice: it withdraws the recommendation, made in this programme's earlier drafts, that a pilot of four to eight units would suffice to detect this pathology.

The correct-step codes saturate and the error codes floor, so a summed technique score can look well spread whilst carrying almost no information about who is at risk. Steps 3 and 5 catch this before the score enters a trial. Therapeutic alliance and consultation coding. The alliance-coding case that follows is likewise speculative: it illustrates where the same architecture could recur, without claiming to have observed it. Alliance and communication-skills tallies routinely include codes for behaviours trained clinicians perform in almost every session (acknowledgement, summarising, open questions), alongside rupture or dismissiveness codes that are rare in recorded, consented sessions. Because the rare codes are exactly the ones carrying the clinically meaningful signal, Step 4 matters most here: a rupture code observed zero times in a pilot is a design finding, rather than an absence of rupture.

saturated | carrying usable variance | floored or degenerate. Note. N = 15 dyads. Codes, with full definitions given in Appendix A: 3ND = affirmative nodding; WT = warm tone; 3EC = mutual eye contact sustained ≥ 3 s; FL = forward lean; 1SL = shared laughter; ST = self-touch or fidget; 3GA = gaze aversion ≥ 3 s; BL = backward lean; S5 = silence ≥ 5 s; MO = monologue by a single speaker ≥ 30 s. Fam. = code family (E = engagement-oriented; W = withdrawal-oriented). Max = protocol-derived theoretical maximum for that code (Section 2.4). % max = mean as a percentage of that code's own maximum. Δ70 / Δ15 = signed distance, in percentage points, from the 70% saturation threshold and from the 15% floor threshold respectively. Status: Saturated = mean ≥ 70% of maximum; Floored = mean ≤ 15% of maximum; Variance = carrying usable between-dyad variance. Ceil. = percentage of dyads scoring at that code's ceiling. n >0 = number of dyads in which the code was observed at least once, of 15. * Monologue was never observed in any dyad and is therefore degenerate (SD = 0) as well as floored. Distances within 10 percentage points of a threshold are bolded, because Section 5 shows these are the codes whose flagging status is unstable under resampling. No codes were excluded. resample_props <-function(X, MAX, n, R) { idx <-matrix(sample.int(nrow(X), n * R, replace = TRUE), nrow = R) sapply(colnames(X), function(k) rowMeans(matrix(X[idx, k], nrow = R)) / MAX[k]) } # The conjunctive screening rule: at least one saturated AND one floored code. screen <-function(P, sat = SAT_CUT, flr = FLOOR_CUT) { n_sat <-rowSums(P >= sat); n_flr <-rowSums(P <= flr) list(detected = n_sat > 0 & n_flr > 0, n_sat = n_sat, n_flr = n_flr) } # One cell of Tables
